Multi-Language Probabilistic Programming
Sam Stites, John M. Li, Steven Holtzen

Abstract
. There are many different probabilistic programming languages that are specialized to specific kinds of probabilistic programs. From a usability and scalability perspective, this is undesirable: today, probabilistic programmers are forced up-front to decide which language they want to use and cannot mix-and-match different languages for handling heterogeneous programs. To rectify this, we seek a foundation for sound interoperability for probabilistic programming languages: just as today’s Python programmers can resort to low-level C programming for performance, we argue that probabilistic programmers should be able to freely mix different languages for meeting the demands of heterogeneous probabilistic programming environments. As a first step towards this goal, we introduce MultiPPL , a probabilistic multi-language that enables programmers to interoperate between two different probabilistic programming languages: one that leverages a high-performance exact discrete inference strategy, and one that uses approximate importance sampling. We give a syntax and semantics for MultiPPL , prove soundness of its inference algorithm, and provide empirical evidence that it enables programmers to perform inference on complex heterogeneous probabilistic programs and flexibly exploits the strengths and weaknesses of two languages simultaneously.

1.
Introduction

Scalable and reliable probabilistic inference remains a significant barrier for
applying and using probabilistic programming languages (PPLs) in practice. The
core of the inference challenge is that there is no universal approach:
different kinds of inference strategies are specialized for different
kinds of probabilistic programs. For example,
Stan
’s inference strategy
is highly effective on continuous and differentiable programs such as
hierarchical Bayesian models, but struggles on programs with high-dimensional
discrete structure such as graph and network reachability
(
Carpenter et al., 2017
)
. On the other extreme, languages
like
Dice
and
ProbLog
scale well on purely-discrete problems,
but the price for this scalability is that they must forego any support
whatsoever of continuous probability
distributions
(
Holtzen et al., 2020
;
Fierens et al., 2015
)
.
In an ideal world, a probabilistic programmer would not have to commit to one language
or the other: they could use a
Dice
-like language for high-performance scalable inference on the
discrete portion of the program, a
Stan
-like language for the portion to which
it is well-suited, and be able to transfer data and control-flow between these
two languages for heterogeneous programs.

This raises a key question: how should we orchestrate the handoff between two
probabilistic programming languages whose underlying semantics may be radically
different and seemingly incompatible? This question of sound language
interoperability has been extensively explored in the context of non-probabilistic
languages
(
Matthews and Findler, 2007
;
Patterson et al., 2022
;
New et al., 2016
;
Tobin-Hochstadt et al., 2011
;
Dagand et al., 2018
;
Scherer et al., 2018
;
Tov and Pucella, 2010
)
,
where the goal is to prove properties such as type-soundness and termination
in a multi-language setting.
As a starting point,
Matthews and Findler (2007)
introduced an effective model for capturing the
interaction between two languages by
language embedding
:
the syntax and operational semantics of a Scheme-like language and an
ML-like language are unified into
a multi-language and syntactic
boundary terms are added to mediate transfer of control and data between the two languages.
Using this embedding approach, they were able to establish the type-soundness of the
multi-language. Their approach
relied on a careful enforcement of soundness properties on the boundaries, for
instance inserting dynamic guards or contracts to ensure soundness when
typed ML values are transferred to untyped Scheme.

We introduce the notion of
sound inference interoperability
:
whereas sound interoperability of traditional languages
ensures that multi-language programs are type-sound,
sound
inference
interoperability ensures
that probabilistic multi-language programs
correctly represent the intended probability distribution.
Our main goal will be to establish sound inference interoperability of two
PPLs: the first is called

Disc
and is similar to
Dice
, and the second is
called

Cont
and it makes use of importance-sampling-based inference. These
two languages are a nice pairing:

Disc
provides scalable exact discrete inference
at the expense of expressivity, as it does not support continuous random
variables and unbounded loops. On the other hand,

Cont
provides significant
expressivity (it supports continuous random variables) at the cost of relying on importance-sampling-based
approximate inference.
Following
Matthews and Findler (2007)
, we embed both

Disc

and

Cont
into a multi-language we call
MultiPPL
.
Together, these two languages cover a broad spectrum of interesting
probabilistic programs that are difficult to handle today.
We will show in
Section

2
and

Section

4
that examples from networking and probabilistic
graphical models benefit from the ability to flexibly use different languages
and inference algorithms within a unified multi-language.
Traditional multi-language semantics establishes sound interoperability
by proving type-soundness of the combined multi-language
(
Matthews and Findler, 2007
)
.
Analogously, we establish sound inference interoperability between

Disc
and

Cont
,
guaranteeing that well-typed
MultiPPL
programs correctly represent
the intended probability distribution. Our contributions are as follows:

•

We introduce
MultiPPL
,
a multi-language in the style of
Matthews and Findler (2007)

that enables interoperation between a discrete exact probabilistic
programming language

Disc
and a continuous approximate probabilistic programming language

Cont
.

•

In
Section

3

we construct two models of
MultiPPL

by combining appropriate semantic domains for

Disc
and

Cont
programs:
a high-level model capturing the probability distribution
intended by a given
MultiPPL
program,
and a low-level model
capturing the details of our particular implementation strategy.
We then prove that these two semantics agree, establishing
correctness of the implementation (
Theorem

3.6
).
We identify two key requirements for ensuring sound inference interoperability between
exact and approximate programs:

Disc
programs must additionally enforce

sample consistency
for ensuring

Disc
values pass safely into

Cont
,
and

Cont
programs must additionally perform

importance weighting
for ensuring the safety of

Disc
conditioning.

•

In
Section

4
we validate the practical effectiveness of
MultiPPL
through our
provided implementation. We evaluate the efficacy of
MultiPPL
by
modeling complex independence structures through real-world problems in the
domain of networking and probabilistic graphical models. We provide insights
into
MultiPPL
’s approach to probabilistic inference and characterize the
nuanced landscape that interoperation produces.

Figure 2
.
Syntax of
MultiPPL
. In

Disc
, we require
{{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e}}\in[0,1]
for

{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{flip}}
. In

Cont
, the syntax of distributions
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}d}
denotes probability distributions. In
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathsf{obs}}
these terms condition, otherwise they are immediately sampled.

We argue that it is often the case that realistic probabilistic programs consist of sub-programs
that are best handled by
different
probabilistic programming languages. Consider for example the

packet arrival

situation visualized in
Fig.

1
.
In this example, at each time step,
network packets are arriving according to a Poisson distribution, a fairly
standard setup in discrete-time queueing theory
(
Meisling, 1958
)
.
Then, each packet is forwarded through the network, whose topology is visualized
as a directed graph. The goal is to query for various properties about the
network’s behavior: for instance, the probability of a packet reaching the end of the network,
or of a packet queue overflowing. This example task is inspired by prior
work on using probabilistic programming languages to perform network
verification
(
Gehr et al., 2018
;
Smolka et al., 2019
)
.

The situation in
Fig.

1
is a small illustrative example of packet arrival,
but programs like it are extremely challenging for today’s PPLs because they
mix different kinds of program structure. Lines 2 and 3 manipulate continuous and
countably-infinite-domain random variables, which precludes the use of
Dice
.
However, graph reachability and queue behavior are complex discrete distributions, which
are difficult for
Stan
due to their inherent non-differentiability and
high-dimensional discrete structure. In order to scale on this example, we would like to be able to use
an inference algorithm like
Stan
’s for lines 2 and 3, and an inference algorithm like

Dice
’s for lines 4–6.

Our approach to designing a language capable of handling
situations like that described in
Fig.

1
is to enable the
programmer to seamlessly transition between programming in two different PPLs:

Cont
, an
expressive language that supports sampling-based inference and continuous random
variables, and

Disc
, a restricted discrete-only language that supports
scalable exact discrete inference. Following
Matthews and Findler (2007)
, we
describe a
probabilistic multi-language
that embeds both
languages into a single unified syntax: see
Fig.

2
. In
Section

3.1

we discuss the intricacies of the syntax in
Fig.

2
in full detail,
including typing judgments found in
Fig.

7
and the appendix; here we briefly
note its high-level structure and discuss examples.

These languages delineate our two syntactic categories:

(1)

Disc
terms, shown in
purple
, that support discrete probabilistic
operations such as Bernoulli random variables and Bayesian conditioning. The
syntax is standard for an ML-like functional language with the addition of
probabilistic constructs:
{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{flip}}~{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e}
introduces a Bernoulli random
variable that is
{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{true}}
with probability
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e}\in[0,1]
and
{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{false}}

otherwise; the construct
{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{observe}}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}M}
conditions on
{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}M}
. Notably,

Disc

lacks introduction forms for continuous random variables or real numbers and
so in order to define the Bernoulli-distributed random variable using

{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{flip}}
, we must rely on interoperation to construct our distribution.

(2)

Cont
terms, shown in
orange
, additionally support standard continuous
operations and sampling capabilities from two distributions inexpressible in

Disc
: a Uniform distribution
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{uniform}}~{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e_{1}}~{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e_{2}}
over the interval

[{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e_{2}}]
, with
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e_{2}}\in\mathbb{R}
, and a Poisson distribution

{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{poisson}}
with rate
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e}\in\mathbb{R}
being greater than zero. The
syntax
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\texttt{obs}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e}_{{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}o}},d)}

denotes conditioning on the event that a sample drawn from distribution
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}d}

is equal to
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e}_{{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}o}}
.

TwoCoins

⬇

1

{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{let}}~{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\theta}~{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{be}}~{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{uniform}}~0~1~{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{in}}~

2

{{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\llparenthesis\,}}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{let}}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}X}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{be}}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{flip}}~{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\theta}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{in}}~

3

{\phantom{\llparenthesis\,}}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{let}}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}Y}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{be}}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{flip}}~{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\theta}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{in}}~

{\phantom{\llparenthesis\,}}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{observe}}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}X\lor Y}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{in}}

5

{\phantom{\llparenthesis\,}}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{ret}}~{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}X}}~{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\,\rrparenthesis_{S}}

Mediating between the

Disc
and

Cont
sublanguages are the boundaries

{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\llparenthesis\,}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e}}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\,\rrparenthesis_{E}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\llparenthesis\,}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}M}}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\,\rrparenthesis_{S}}
: the boundary
{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\llparenthesis\,}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e}}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\,\rrparenthesis_{E}}
allows
passing from

Cont
to

Disc
, and the boundary
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\llparenthesis\,}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}M}}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\,\rrparenthesis_{S}}
allows passing
from

Disc
to

Cont
. This style of presentation is similar to
Patterson (2022)
.

shows an example program in our multi-language which
passes a uniformly-sampled real value
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\theta}
from

Cont
into

Disc

and uses the resulting value as a prior for sampling two independent Bernoulli
random variables.
The outer-most language is

Cont
. On
Line

1
,
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\theta}

is bound to a sample drawn from the uniform distribution on the unit interval.
Then, on Lines
2
–
5
, we begin evaluation of
a

Disc
program inside the boundary term
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\llparenthesis\,}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}-}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\,\rrparenthesis_{S}}

. We flip two coins

{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}X}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}Y}
(
Lines

2
and
3
, respectively)
in the

Disc
sub-language, whose prior parameters are both
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\theta}
.
On
Line

4
, we observe that one of the two coins was true,
taking advantage of syntactic sugar where
{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{observe}}
is bound to a discarded
variable name.

Line

5
brings us to the final line of our program, where we
query for the probability that
{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}X}
is true.
The next
two sub-sections will explain our approach to bridging
the two languages.

2.1.
Disc
and

Cont
inference

Before we describe the intricacies of language interoperation, we first provide
some high-level intuition for how we wish to perform inference on

Disc
and

Cont
independently.
First, we give a denotational semantics for
MultiPPL
that we denote
\left\llbracket{-}\right\rrbracket

which associates each
MultiPPL
term with a probability distribution on

MultiPPL
values (see
Section

3
for a formal definition of these
semantics). Here we will briefly illustrate these semantics by example: the semantics

\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{flip}}~{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}p}}\right\rrbracket
produces a Bernoulli distribution that is true with probability
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}p}\in[0,1]
;
the semantics
\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{uniform}}~{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e_{1}}~{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e_{2}}}\right\rrbracket
produces a uniform distribution on the interval
[{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e_{2}}]\in\mathbb{R}
.

The goal of inference is to efficiently evaluate the denotation of a probabilistic
program. While

Disc
and

Cont
share a unified denotation, they have
very different approaches to inference.
The key advantage of our multi-language approach is that
we can specialize the design of

Cont
and

Disc
to take full advantage of
structural differences between their underlying inference algorithms: for

Disc
we will use an exact inference strategy based on knowledge compilation
similar to
Dice

(
Holtzen et al., 2020
)
, and for

Cont
we will rely on
approximate inference via sampling.
In the next two subsections we give a high-level overview of these standard
approaches.

2.1.1.
Exact inference via knowledge compilation

⬇

1

{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{let}}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}X}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{be}}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{flip}}~0.4~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{in}}~

2

{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{let}}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}Y}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{be}}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{flip}}~0.3~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{in}}~

3

{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{observe}}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}X\lor Y}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{in}}

4

{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{ret}}~{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}X}}

(a)

Example

Disc
program.

f_{X}
T
F
0.4
0.6
f_{X}
f_{Y}
T
T
F
0.6
0.4
0.3
0.7

(b)

BDD representations of formulas.

Figure 3
.
Motivating example showing the compilation of the

Disc
program in
3(a)
to BDDs in
3(b)
.
On the left of
3(b)
is a BDD representing the distribution formula
\varphi=f_{X}
; on the right
is the BDD representing the accepting formula
\alpha=f_{X}\lor f_{Y}
.
T
and
F
represent true and false values, respectively.

Here, we illustrate the principles of exact inference in

Disc
via example;

Section

3
provides a formal treatment of these semantics.
In
Fig.

3(a)
, we reproduce the

Disc
program compiled in
Lines
3
–
5
of
, but
instantiate the priors of
{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}Y}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}Z}
with numeric literals 0.4 and
0.3, respectively.
Our example in
Fig.

3(a)
denotes the
probability distribution of Bernoulli 0.3, given that one of the two weighted
coin flips is true; its semantics is
\left\llbracket{\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{fig:etwocoins:program}}\right\rrbracket(\mathrm{true})=\frac{0.4}{0.58}\approx 0.689
.

The exact inference strategy used by

Disc
is to perform probabilistic
inference via weighted model
counting
(
Darwiche and Marquis, 2002
;
Sang et al., 2005
;
Chavira and Darwiche, 2008
)
,
following
Holtzen et al. (2020)
.
The key idea is to interpret the probabilistic program
as a weighted Boolean formula whose models are in one-to-one
correspondence with paths through the program, and where each path is associated
with a weight that matches the probability of that path in the program.
Concretely,
a
weighted Boolean formula
is a
pair
(\varphi,w)
where
\varphi
is a Boolean formula and
w
is a

weight function
that associates literals (assignments to variables) in

\varphi
with real-valued weights. Then, the
weighted model count

of a weighted Boolean formula is the weighted sum of models:

(1)

\displaystyle\textsf{WMC}(\varphi,w)=\sum_{\{m\vDash\varphi\}}\prod_{\{\ell\in m\}}w(\ell).

To perform

Disc
inference by reduction to weighted model counting, we
associate each

Disc
program with a pair of Boolean formulae in a manner similar to

Holtzen et al. (2020)
: (1) an
accepting formula

\alpha
that encodes the
paths through the program that does not violate observations; and (2)
a
distribution formula

\varphi
such that
\textsf{WMC}(\varphi\land\alpha)

is the unnormalized probability of the program returning
\mathrm{true}
.
For instance, we would compile
Fig.

3(a)
into accepting formula
\varphi=f_{X}

and
\alpha=f_{X}\lor f_{Y}
, where
f_{X}
is a Boolean variable that represents the outcome
of
{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{flip}}~0.4
and
f_{Y}
represents the outcome of
{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{flip}}~0.3
. Then, the weight
function is
w(f_{X})=0.4,w(\overline{f_{X}})=0.6,w(f_{Y})=0.4,w(f_{Y})=0.3,w(\overline{f_{Y}})=0.7
.
Then, we can compute the semantics of
Fig.

3(a)
by performing
weighted model counting:

\displaystyle\left\llbracket{\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{fig:etwocoins:program}}\right\rrbracket(\mathrm{true})=\frac{\textsf{WMC}(\varphi\wedge\alpha,w)}{\textsf{WMC}(\alpha,w)}=\frac{\textsf{WMC}(f_{X},w)}{\textsf{WMC}(f_{X}\lor f_{Y},w)}=\frac{0.4}{0.4+0.6\cdot 0.3}=\frac{0.4}{0.58}\approx 0.689

The weighted model counting task is well-studied, and there is an array of
high-performance implementations for solving
it
(
Holtzen et al., 2020
;
Chavira and Darwiche, 2008
;
Sang et al., 2005
)
. One
approach that is particularly effective is
knowledge compilation
, which
compiles the Boolean formula into a representation for which weighted model
counting can be performed efficiently (typically, polynomial-time in the size of
the compiled representation). A common target for this compilation process is

binary decision diagrams
(BDDs), shown in
Fig.

3(b)
.
A BDD is a rooted DAG whose internal nodes are labeled with
Boolean variables and whose leaves are labeled with either true or false values.
A BDD is read top-down: solid edges denote true assignments to variables,
and dashed edges denote false assignments.
Once a Boolean
formula is compiled to a BDD, inference can be performed in polynomial time (in the
size of the BDD) by performing a bottom-up traversal of the DAG.

While highly effective for discrete probabilistic inference tasks with finite domains,
inference via knowledge compilation has a critical weakness: it cannot support continuous
random variables or unbounded discrete random variables due to the requirement that each
program be associated with a (finite) Boolean formula. Hence, the design of

Disc

must be carefully restricted to only permit programs that can be compiled
to Boolean formulae, which is why it does not contain syntactic support for these features.

2.1.2.
Approximate inference via sampling

A powerful alternative to exact inference is approximate inference via
sampling. The engine that drives sampling-based inference is the
expectation estimator.
The expectation estimator is widely used as a foundation for approximate
inference strategies for probabilistic
programs
(
Carpenter et al., 2017
;
Mansinghka et al., 2014
;
Staton et al., 2016
;
Ramsey and Pfeffer, 2002
;
Murray et al., 2018
;
Culpepper and Cobb, 2017
)
.
We will use it to give an inference algorithm for

Cont
.
Concretely, suppose we want to use the
expectation estimator to approximate the semantics of the

Cont
program

\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{flip}}~1/4}\right\rrbracket
. To do this, we can draw
N=100
samples
from the program: in roughly
1/4
of these samples, the program will output
\mathrm{true}
.
This approach is known as
direct sampling
, and is one way of utilizing the expectation
estimator to design approximate inference algorithms.

Formally, let
\Omega
be a sample space,
\Pr
a probability density function, and
let
X:\Omega\rightarrow\real
be a real-valued random variable out of the sample
space. Then, the expectation of
X
is defined as

\mathbb{E}_{\Pr}[X]=\int\Pr(\omega)X(\omega)d\omega
.
The
expectation estimator
approximates
the expectation of a random variable
X
by drawing
N
samples from
\Pr
:

(2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\Pr}[X]\approx\frac{1}{N}\sum_{x\sim\Pr}^{N}X(x).

There are many more advanced
approaches to sampling-based inference beyond direct sampling such as
Hamiltonian Monte-Carlo
(
Carpenter et al., 2017
;
Neal, 2011
)
; at their core,
all these approximate inference algorithms follow the same principle of drawing some number of samples
from the program and using that to estimate the semantics.

When compared with the exact inference strategy described in
Section

2.1.1
,
sampling-based inference has the key advantage that it only requires the ability
to sample from the probabilistic program: each time a random quantity is
introduced, it can be dealt with by eagerly sampling. This makes sampling an
ideal inference algorithm for implementing flexible and expressive languages
with many features: unlike

Disc
, it is straightforward to add interesting
features like continuous random variable and unbounded loops to

Cont
without
wholesale redesigning of its inference algorithm. This gain in expressivity
comes at the cost of precision: unlike

Disc
,

Cont
is only able to provide
an approximation to the final expectation.

⬇

1

{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{let}}~{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}x}~{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{be}}~{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{flip}}~0.20~{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{in}}~

2

{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\llparenthesis\,}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{let}}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}Y}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{be}}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{flip}}~0.25~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{in}}~

3

\phantom{\llparenthesis\,}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{observe}}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\llparenthesis\,}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}x}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\,\rrparenthesis_{E}}\lor Y}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{in}}

4

\phantom{\llparenthesis\,}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{ret}}~{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}Y}}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\,\rrparenthesis_{S}}

(a)

Motivating example.

⬇

1

{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\llparenthesis\,}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{let}}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}Y}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{be}}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{flip}}~0.25~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{in}}~

2

\phantom{\llparenthesis\,}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{observe}}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{true}}\lor Y}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{in}}

\phantom{\llparenthesis\,}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{ret}}~{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}Y}}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\,\rrparenthesis_{S}}

(b)

Sampled
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}x}=\mathrm{true}
.

⬇

1

{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\llparenthesis\,}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{let}}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}Y}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{be}}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{flip}}~0.25~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{in}}~

2

\phantom{\llparenthesis\,}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{observe}}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{false}}\lor Y}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{in}}

3

\phantom{\llparenthesis\,}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{ret}}~{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}Y}}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\,\rrparenthesis_{S}}

(c)

Sampled
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}x}=\mathrm{false}
.

Figure 4
.
Interpreting

Cont
values in

Disc
.

2.2.
Sound interoperation

Now we move on to our main goal, establishing sound interoperation between
the underlying inference strategies of

Disc
and

Cont
by identifying two key invariants that must be maintained
when transporting

Disc
and

Cont
values across boundaries:
importance
weighting
and
sample consistency
.
At first, there appears to be a straightforward way of establishing interoperation
between these two languages: when a

Cont
value
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}v_{s}}
is interpreted in
a

Disc
context, it is lifted in a Dirac-delta distribution on
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}v_{s}}
.

Figure

4(a)
gives an illustration of this scenario: first, on
Line

1

we sample a value (either
\mathrm{true}
or
\mathrm{false}
) for
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}x}
. Then, on
Line

3
we
interpret
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}x}
within an exact context.
Figures

4(b)
and
4(c)

show the two possible liftings: the sampled value is given its straightforward interpretation
in the exact context.
When a

Disc
value
{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}v_{e}}
is interpreted in a

Cont
context,
one can draw a sample
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}v_{s}}
from the
exact
distribution denoted by
{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}v_{e}}
.
However, we will show in the next two sub-sections that a naive approach that fails to preserve key
invariants will result in incorrect inference results, and that one must maintain
careful invariants in order to ensure soundness of inference across boundaries.

2.2.1.
Importance weighting

Let us more carefully
consider the situation shown in
Fig.

4(a)
. First, we observe that
the desired semantics is
\left\llbracket{\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{fig:interop1:a}}\right\rrbracket(\mathrm{true})=0.25/0.4=0.625
. Suppose we were to follow
a naive multi-language inference procedure of drawing 100 samples
by eagerly evaluating values for
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}x}
. Following
Section

2.1.2
, approximately 20 of these samples will yield the program in

Fig.

4(b)
and approximately 80 will yield the program in
Fig.

4(c)
.
Observe that

\left\llbracket{\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{fig:interop1:b}}\right\rrbracket(\mathrm{true})=0.25
and
\left\llbracket{\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{fig:interop1:c}}\right\rrbracket(\mathrm{true})=1
. So
our naive estimate
\left\llbracket{\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{fig:interop1:a}}\right\rrbracket(\mathrm{true})
would be:

(3)

\displaystyle\left\llbracket{\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{fig:interop1:a}}\right\rrbracket(\mathrm{true})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle?}}{{\approx}}\frac{20}{100}\left\llbracket{\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{fig:interop1:b}}\right\rrbracket(\mathrm{true})+\frac{80}{100}\left\llbracket{\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{fig:interop1:c}}\right\rrbracket(\mathrm{true})=0.85

Something went wrong – we expected the result of
Eq.

3
to be 0.625. This naive
sampling approach significantly over-estimated
\left\llbracket{\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{fig:interop1:a}}\right\rrbracket(\mathrm{true})
.
The issue is that, in this naive approach, the observation that occurs on

Line

3
(
Fig.

4(a)
) is not taken into account when
sampling a value for
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}x}
: samples where
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}x}=\mathrm{true}
are under-sampled
relative to their true probability, and samples where
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}x}=\mathrm{false}
are
over-sampled.

This example illustrates that a naive approach to interoperation is unsound. To fix it,
one approach is to adjust the
relative importance
of each sample: we will
still sample
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}x}=\mathrm{true}
roughly 20% of the time, but we will decrease the overall
importance of this sample. The key idea comes from
importance sampling
, which is a
refinement of the expectation estimator given in
Eq.

2
but enables estimating
an expectation
\mathbb{E}_{p}[X]
by sampling from a
proposal distribution

q
:

(4)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{p}[X]=\int X(x)p(x)dx=\int X(x)\frac{p(x)}{q(x)}q(x)dx=\mathbb{E}_{q}\left[X(x)\frac{p(x)}{q(x)}\right].

The above holds as long as the proposal
q

supports

p
(i.e., satisfies
the property that, for all
x
, if
p(x)>0
then
q(x)>0
). The ratio

p(x)/q(x)
is called the
importance weight
of the sample
x
:
intuitively, if
x
is more likely according to the true distribution
p
than
the proposal
q
, the importance ratio will be greater than 1; similarly, if

x
is less likely according to
p
than
q
, its weight will be less than 1.
In this instance, the proposal
q
is semantics of the program with all observe
statements deleted, and
p
is
\left\llbracket{\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{fig:interop1:a}}\right\rrbracket
.

Unfortunately, already having access to
p
defeats the purpose of
approximating. In addition, our programs
p
always incorporate a normalization
constant
Z
, such that

(5)

p(x)=\hat{p}(x)/Z,

with
\hat{p}
being the unnormalized distribution. Summing the probability of

\hat{p}
for all
x
in the domain of
p
yields

Z=\int\hat{p}(x)dx
. Computing this normalization constant is
expensive, and amounts to calculating
p
directly. In our setting, calculating
this normalization constant is identical to the
denotation of
Line

3
in the exact setting. To avoid solving
for this in our importance sampler, we can incorporate
Eq.

5
into our expectation

Eq.

4
and jointly approximate our query alongside
Z
,

(6)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{p}[X]=\int X(x)p(x)dx=\frac{\int X(x)\hat{p}(x)dx}{\int\hat{p}(x)dx}=\frac{\int X(x)\frac{\hat{p}(x)}{q(x)}q(x)dx}{\int\frac{\hat{p}(x)}{q(x)}q(x)dx}=\frac{\mathbb{E}_{x\sim q}\left[X(x)\frac{\hat{p}(x)}{q(x)}\right]}{\mathbb{E}_{x\sim q}\left[\frac{\hat{p}(x)}{q(x)}\right]}.

The above is called a
self-normalized importance sampler

(
Robert et al., 1999
)
.
Here, in the denominator, we construct the normalizing constant for
q
to be the ratio of the unnormalized
\hat{p}
to
q
: the
Line

2
in

Fig.

4(b)
when
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}x}=\mathrm{true}
and the
Line

2

in
Fig.

4(c)
when
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}x}=\mathrm{false}
. Notice that the probability of evidence encoded by
{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{observe}}
statements in
Fig.

4(b)
and
Fig.

4(b)
are

\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathrm{true}\lor Y}}\right\rrbracket(\mathrm{true})=1
and

\left\llbracket{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathrm{false}\lor Y}}\right\rrbracket(\mathrm{true})=0.25
, respectively.

Sampling 100 draws of
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}x}
, again, with 20 samples yielding the program in

Fig.

4(b)
and 80 samples yielding the program in
Fig.

4(c)
,
Eq.

6
now returns our expected result:

\displaystyle\left\llbracket{\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{fig:interop1:a}}\right\rrbracket(\mathrm{true})\approx\frac{\frac{20}{100}\,1\cdot\left\llbracket{\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{fig:interop1:b}}\right\rrbracket(\mathrm{true})+\frac{80}{100}\,0.25\cdot\left\llbracket{\lx@cref{creftype~refnum}{fig:interop1:c}}\right\rrbracket(\mathrm{true})}{\frac{20}{100}\,1+\frac{80}{100}\,0.25}=\frac{20\cdot 0.25+80\cdot 0.25}{20+20}=0.625

2.2.2.
Sample consistency

Sample consistency

⬇

1

{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{let}}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}X}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{be}}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{flip}}~0.5~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{in}}~

2

{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\llparenthesis\,}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{let}}~{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}y}~{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{be}}~{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\llparenthesis\,}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}X}}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\,\rrparenthesis_{S}}~{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{in}}~

3

\phantom{\llparenthesis\,}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{let}}~{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}z}~{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{be}}~{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\llparenthesis\,}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}X}}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\,\rrparenthesis_{S}}~{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{in}}~

4

\phantom{\llparenthesis\,}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{ret}}~{{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}y\land z}}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\,\rrparenthesis_{E}}

Importance weighting is not all that is necessary to ensure sound interoperability:
we must also ensure that

Disc
values are safely interpreted with a

Cont
context.
Consider the example in
. There are two observations to make
about this program. The first is that we embed a

Cont
program into a

Disc

context; this results in a sampler that evaluates all

Cont
fragments while
preserving the semantics of all

Disc
variables in order to produce a sample.
The next thing to notice is that a

Disc
program denotes a distribution; in
the semantics of

Cont
, when we come across a distribution a sample is
immediately drawn from it.

Again, we can propose a naive strategy for performing inference on this program:
one where we draw a new sample each time
we encounter a distribution. Notice that Line 2 holds a reference
{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}X}
to

{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{flip}}~0.5
, denoting a Bernoulli distribution. When we evaluate this boundary,
with probability
1/2
we sample
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}y}=\mathrm{true}
; suppose we sample
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}y}=\mathrm{true}
. We encounter this reference,
again, on Line 3 and suppose we sample
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}z}=\mathrm{false}
.
Finally, on Line 4, we evaluate the Boolean expression, resulting in
\mathrm{false}
,
which is lifted into the Dirac-delta distribution in

Disc
. Running this
program
n
number of times, we will expect to see the expectation of

{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}y\land z}
with
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}y}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}z}
as two independent draws of the
fair Bernoulli distribution.
At this point, something strange has occurred: by referencing a single variable
in

Disc
, we have simulated two independent
{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{flip}}
s.

Intuitively, the sampled value for
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}z}
must be

the same
as the sampled value for
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}y}
. Operationally, to ensure this
is the case, any samples drawn across at the
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\llparenthesis\,}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}-}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\,\rrparenthesis_{S}}
boundary additionally
constraining

Disc
program’s accepting criteria so that all subsequent samples
remain consistent.

3.
MultiPPL
: Multi-Language Probabilistic Programming

In this section we present
MultiPPL
,
a multilanguage that supports both exact and sampling-based inference.

Sections

3.1
and
3.2
describe the syntax of
MultiPPL
programs
and
MultiPPL
’s type system.
We then present two semantic models of
MultiPPL
.
First,
Section

3.3
presents a high-level model
\mathcal{H}\!\left\llbracket\hskip 0.50003pt-\hskip 0.50003pt\right\rrbracket

capturing the probability distribution generated by a
MultiPPL

program; this model specifies the intended behavior of our implementation.
Second,
Section

3.4
presents a low-level model
\mathcal{L}\!\left\llbracket\hskip 0.50003pt-\hskip 0.50003pt\right\rrbracket

capturing our particular inference strategy; taking the intuition we have built up in
Section

2.2
and providing the precise way in which our implementation
combines knowledge compilation with importance sampling.
Finally,

Section

3.5
connects these two models:
we show that
\mathcal{L}\!\left\llbracket\hskip 0.50003pt-\hskip 0.50003pt\right\rrbracket
soundly refines
\mathcal{H}\!\left\llbracket\hskip 0.50003pt-\hskip 0.50003pt\right\rrbracket
,
establishing sound inference interoperability between

Disc
and

Cont

with respect to our inference strategy.

3.1.
Syntax

The syntax of
MultiPPL
is given in
Fig.

2
.

MultiPPL
is a union of two sublanguages,

Disc
and

Cont
, that support exact and sampling-based inference.
To streamline
the presentation of the models in
Sections

3.3
and
3.4
,
each sublanguage is then subdivided into
pure and effectful fragments.

The sampling-based sublanguage

Cont
is a first-order probabilistic
programming language with Booleans, tuples, and real numbers. In

Cont
, the
pure fragment includes not only the basic operations on Booleans and pairs, but
also arithmetic operations on real numbers. The effectful fragment
additionally includes primitive operations
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{uniform}}~{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e_{1}}~{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e_{2}}
for
generating uniformly-distributed real numbers in the interval
[{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e_{1}},{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e_{2}}]
,

{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{poisson}}~{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e}
for generating Poisson-distributed integers with rate
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e}
,
and
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathsf{obs}}
-expressions denoting conditioning operators for these
distributions.

The exact sublanguage

Disc
, reminiscent of
Dice

(
Holtzen et al., 2020
)
,
is a discrete first-order probabilistic programming language with Booleans and tuples.
The pure fragment of

Disc
includes the basic operations on Booleans and
pairs, while the effectful fragment includes constructs for sequencing and
branching, as well as the primitive operations
{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{flip}}~{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e}
– for
generating Bernoulli-distributed Booleans with parameter
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e}
of type
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{real}}
,
and
{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{observe}}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}M}
– for conditioning on an event
{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}M}
.

The

Disc

branching construct
{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{if}}~{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{then}}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}M}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{else}}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}N}
requires the guard
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e}
to be a

Cont
term.
This is not an essential restriction, but rather
required for sound inference interoperability
with respect to the specific implementation strategy we have chosen.
As sketched in
Sections

2.1.1
and
2.1.2
,
standard sampling-based inference maintains a weight for the current trace,
while exact inference maintains a weight map and an accepting formula.
In our implementation, we wanted a language whose inference algorithm
would stay as close to these traditional
inference algorithms as possible while avoiding incorrect weighting schemes.
To do this while maintaining safe inference interoperability,
one must have the rather subtle invariant
that
{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{if}}{\text{-}}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{then}}{\text{-}}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{else}}
expressions in the

Disc

sublanguage have then- and else- branches that importance-weight
their respective traces by the same amount.
The syntactic restriction on
{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{if}}~{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{then}}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}M}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{else}}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}N}
is a simple way of
ensuring this is always the case: probabilistic choice is removed, and only one branch need ever be considered.
In our implementation, we also permit
{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{if}}{\text{-}}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{then}}{\text{-}}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{else}}
expressions
where both branches are boundary-free

Disc
programs, as exact inference
for such programs can be performed just as in
Holtzen et al. (2020)
, without
touching the importance weight.
These special cases could
be avoided by maintaining
an auxiliary Boolean formula tracking a
path condition
,
which encodes during inference the then- and else- branches
of
{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{if}}{\text{-}}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{then}}{\text{-}}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{else}}
expressions taken to reach a given subterm.
This would allow arbitrary
{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{if}}{\text{-}}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{then}}{\text{-}}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{else}}
expressions in the

Disc

sublanguage, at the expense of additional overhead of maintaining
this path condition during inference.
In our design of
MultiPPL
, we have decided to restrict the
syntax of the language rather than impose a performance cost;
in practice, this has been sufficient to express
all of the examples in
Section

4
.

3.2.
Typing

{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}A}\mathrel{\leftrightsquigarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\tau}

\frac{~}{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{unit}}\mathrel{\leftrightsquigarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{unit}}}\frac{~}{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{bool}}\mathrel{\leftrightsquigarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{bool}}}\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}A}\mathrel{\leftrightsquigarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\tau}\hskip 9.24994pt{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}B}\mathrel{\leftrightsquigarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\sigma}}{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}A}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\times}}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}B}\mathrel{\leftrightsquigarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\tau}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\times}}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\sigma}}

.

types

{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\tau}

9.24994pt{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e}:{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{real}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\Gamma};{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\Delta}\vdash_{\rm c}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathsf{obs}(}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e_{o}}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0},}~{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{poisson}}~{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0})}:{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{unit}}}\par\par\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\Gamma};{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\Delta}\vdash_{\rm c}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}M}:{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}A}\hskip 9.24994pt{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}A}\mathrel{\leftrightsquigarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\tau}}{{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\Gamma};{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\Delta}\vdash_{\rm c}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\llparenthesis\,}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}M}}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\,\rrparenthesis_{S}}:{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\tau}}

Figure 6
.
Typing rules for the effectful fragment of
MultiPPL
.

The syntax of types and typing contexts is given in
Fig.

2
.

Disc
types
{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}A}
include Booleans and pairs;

Cont
types
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\tau}
additionally include a type of real numbers.
A

Disc
typing context
{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\Delta}
is a mapping of

Disc
variables to

Disc
types,
and a

Cont
typing context
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\Gamma}
is a mapping of

Cont
variables to

Cont
types.
By convention we will denote

Disc
syntactic elements with capital
letters and

Cont
elements with lower-case Greek letters. This section is best read
in color, where we use orange monotype font for

Cont
terms and purple
sans-serif font for

Disc
terms.

MultiPPL
contains two sublanguages that each have a pure and effectful part,
so there are correspondingly four forms of typing judgment. For the pure fragments,

•

{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\Delta}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}M}:{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}A}
says the pure

Disc
term
{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}M}
has

Disc
type
{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}A}
in

Disc
context
{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\Delta}
.

•

{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e}:{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\tau}
says the pure

Cont
term
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e}
has

Cont
type
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\tau}
in

Cont
context
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\Gamma}
.

These judgments are standard and deferred to the appendix.

The typing judgments for effectful
MultiPPL
terms
are parameterized by a combined context
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\Gamma};{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\Delta}
,
as an effectful term may mention variables
from both

Disc
and

Cont
via boundaries:

•

{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\Gamma};{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\Delta}\vdash_{\rm c}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}M}:{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}A}
says the effectful

Disc
term
{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}M}
has

Disc
type
{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}A}
in combined context
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\Gamma};{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\Delta}
.

•

{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\Gamma};{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\Delta}\vdash_{\rm c}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e}:{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\tau}
says the effectful

Cont
term
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e}
has

Cont
type
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\tau}
in combined context
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\Gamma};{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\Delta}
.

These judgments are defined in
Fig.

6
.
Note that in the rule
for
{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{flip}}~{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e}
, the parameter
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e}

can be an arbitrary pure

Cont
term; this allows expressing the
TwoCoins
example
from
Section

2
.
In principle, one could allow arbitrary effectful

Cont
programs
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e}
as
parameter to
{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{flip}}
instead of just pure ones, but we have not found this to
be useful in practice.
The typing judgments for the boundaries
{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\llparenthesis\,}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e}}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\,\rrparenthesis_{E}}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\llparenthesis\,}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}M}}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\,\rrparenthesis_{S}}

allow converting

Disc
terms of type
{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}A}
into

Cont
terms of type
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\tau}

and vice versa,
so long as
{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}A}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\tau}

are
convertible
,
written
{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}A}\mathrel{\leftrightsquigarrow}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\tau}
.
The convertibility relation
is defined in

Fig.

5
; it simply states that

Disc
types can be converted into
their

Cont
counterparts in the expected way,
and that the

Cont
type
{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{real}}
has no

Disc
counterpart.

3.3.
High-level semantic model

\begin{aligned} \left\llbracket\hskip 0.42502pt{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{unit}}\hskip 0.42502pt\right\rrbracket&=\{\star\}\\
\left\llbracket\hskip 0.42502pt{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{bool}}\hskip 0.42502pt\right\rrbracket&=\{\top,\bot\}\\
\left\llbracket\hskip 0.42502pt{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}A}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\times}}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}B}\hskip 0.42502pt\right\rrbracket&=\left\llbracket\hskip 0.42502pt{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}A}\hskip 0.42502pt\right\rrbracket\times\left\llbracket\hskip 0.42502pt{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}B}\hskip 0.42502pt\right\rrbracket\end{aligned}\hskip 8.50012pt\begin{aligned} \left\llbracket\hskip 0.42502pt{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{unit}}\hskip 0.42502pt\right\rrbracket&=\{\star\}\\
\left\llbracket\hskip 0.42502pt{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{bool}}\hskip 0.42502pt\right\rrbracket&=\{\top,\bot\}\\
\left\llbracket\hskip 0.42502pt{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{real}}\hskip 0.42502pt\right\rrbracket&=\mathbb{R}\\
\left\llbracket\hskip 0.42502pt{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\sigma}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\times}}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\tau}\hskip 0.42502pt\right\rrbracket&=\left\llbracket\hskip 0.42502pt{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\sigma}\hskip 0.42502pt\right\rrbracket\times\left\llbracket\hskip 0.42502pt{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\tau}\hskip 0.42502pt\right\rrbracket\end{aligned}\hskip 8.50012pt\begin{aligned} &\left\llbracket\hskip 0.42502pt{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\Delta}\hskip 0.42502pt\right\rrbracket=\prod_{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}X}\in\operatorname{dom}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\Delta}}\left\llbracket\hskip 0.42502pt{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\Delta}({\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}X})\hskip 0.42502pt\right\rrbracket\end{aligned}\hskip 8.50012pt\begin{aligned} \left\llbracket\hskip 0.42502pt{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\Gamma}\hskip 0.42502pt\right\rrbracket&=\prod_{{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}x}\in\operatorname{dom}{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\Gamma}}\left\llbracket\hskip 0.42502pt{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\Gamma}({\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}x})\hskip 0.42502pt\right\rrbracket\end{aligned}

Interpreting types and typing contexts.

\begin{aligned} \left\llbracket\hskip 0.42502pt{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\Delta}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}M}:{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}A}\hskip 0.42502pt\right\rrbracket\!&:\left\llbracket\hskip 0.42502pt{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\Delta}\hskip 0.42502pt\right\rrbracket\to\left\llbracket\hskip 0.42502pt{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}A}\hskip 0.42502pt\right\rrbracket\\
\left\llbracket\hskip 0.42502pt{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}X}\hskip 0.42502pt\right\rrbracket\!(\delta)&=\delta({\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}X})\\
\left\llbracket\hskip 0.42502pt{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{true}}\hskip 0.42502pt\right\rrbracket\!(\delta)&=\top\\
\left\llbracket\hskip 0.42502pt{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{false}}\hskip 0.42502pt\right\rrbracket\!(\delta)&=\bot\\
\left\llbracket\hskip 0.42502pt{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}M}\mathbin{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{\land}}}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}N}\hskip 0.42502pt\right\rrbracket\!(\delta)&=\begin{cases}\top,&\text{if }\left\llbracket\hskip 0.42502pt{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}M}\hskip 0.42502pt\right\rrbracket\!(\delta)=\left\llbracket\hskip 0.42502pt{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}N}\hskip 0.42502pt\right\rrbracket\!(\delta)=\top\\

\left\llbracket\hskip 0.42502pt\mathop{{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{\neg}}}{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}M}\hskip 0.42502pt\right\rrbracket\!(\delta)&=\begin{cases}\bot,&\text{if }\left\llbracket\hskip 0.42502pt{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}M}\hskip 0.42502pt\right\rrbracket\!(\delta)=\top\\

\left\llbracket\hskip 0.42502pt{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}\mathsf{snd}}~{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}M}\hskip 0.42502pt\right\rrbracket\!(\delta)&=\pi_{2}(\left\llbracket\hskip 0.42502pt{\color[rgb]{0.4688,0.3672,0.9414}M}\hskip 0.42502pt\right\rrbracket\!(\delta))\end{aligned}\hskip 17.00024pt\begin{aligned} \left\llbracket\hskip 0.42502pt{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\Gamma}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}e}:{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\tau}\hskip 0.42502pt\right\rrbracket\!&:\left\llbracket\hskip 0.42502pt{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\Gamma}\hskip 0.42502pt\right\rrbracket\xrightarrow{\rm measurable}{\left\llbracket\hskip 0.42502pt{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\tau}\hskip 0.42502pt\right\rrbracket}\\

\left\llbracket\hskip 0.42502pt{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{true}}\hskip 0.42502pt\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma)&=\top\\
\left\llbracket\hskip 0.42502pt{\color[rgb]{0.9961,0.3789,0}\mathtt{false}}\hskip 0.42502pt\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma)&=\bot\\
\left\llbracket\hskip 0.42502ptr\hskip 0.42502pt\right\rrbracket\!(\gamma)&=r\\
